165	
166	\paragraph{Cross-configuration selection analysis.}
167	Table~\ref{tab:cross_selection} extends the selection analysis to four additional configurations.
168	On LLaMA SciERC, where output diversity remains substantial (18\% identical outputs, 39\% unique), LP achieves the highest selection F1 (0.652) at $N{=}8$ but still falls within bootstrap noise of greedy (0.646), replicating the gap observed on Qwen SciERC (Table~\ref{tab:selection}).
169	On CoNLL, 57--88\% of instances produce identical outputs across all $N$ samples, reflecting a ceiling effect (greedy F1 ${\approx}$0.91--0.92) rather than signal failure; selection analysis on these configurations is uninformative because there is little per-sample variance for any signal to exploit.
170	
171	\begin{table*}[t]
172	\centering
173	\small
174	\caption{Best-of-$N$ selection F1 across configurations ($T{=}1.0$). SJ: Soft Jaccard, FK: Fleiss' $\kappa$, EM: Exact Match, LP: Log-prob, VC: Voting Confidence. Best non-oracle signal per row in \textbf{bold}. No signal significantly outperforms greedy in any configuration; CoNLL shows especially tight clustering where greedy already exceeds 0.90.}
175	\label{tab:cross_selection}
176	\begin{tabular}{llccccccc}
177	\toprule
178	Config & $n$ & Greedy & Oracle & SJ & FK & EM & LP & VC \\
179	\midrule
180	LLaMA/SciERC $N{=}8$ & 529 & .646 & .778 & .643 & .639 & .634 & \textbf{.652} & .633 \\
181	LLaMA/SciERC $N{=}16$ & 529 & .645 & .806 & \textbf{.649} & \textbf{.649} & .640 & .646 & .640 \\
182	Qwen/CoNLL $N{=}8$ & 2{,}756 & .908 & .948 & \textbf{.899} & .893 & .898 & .897 & .895 \\
183	LLaMA/CoNLL $N{=}8$ & 2{,}756 & .924 & .943 & .924 & \textbf{.925} & .924 & .924 & .923 \\
184	\bottomrule
185	\end{tabular}
186	\end{table*}
187	
188	\paragraph{Diminishing returns of $N$-scaling.}
189	Our $N{=}8$ and $N{=}16$ comparisons (Table~\ref{tab:robustness}) characterize the marginal value of additional samples for test-time scaling \cite{brown2024monkeys, snell2024scaling}.
190	For quality estimation, structural signals benefit consistently but sublinearly: SJ $\rho$ improves by $+0.052$ (Qwen SciERC, 3-seed mean) and $+0.032$ (LLaMA CoNLL, 3-seed mean) when doubling from $N{=}8$ to $N{=}16$, while log-probability is approximately $N$-invariant ($\Delta\rho{\leq}0.021$).
191	Structural signals leverage $\binom{N}{2}$ pairwise comparisons (28 pairs at $N{=}8$, 120 at $N{=}16$), but the $\rho$ improvement is far less than proportional, indicating diminishing information per additional comparison.
192	This asymmetry gives structural signals a relative advantage in the compute-constrained regime: at $N{=}8$, SJ already achieves $\rho{=}0.431$ on LLaMA CoNLL (vs.\ LP $\rho{=}0.307$, $\Delta{=}{+}0.124$), and the gap \textit{widens} at $N{=}16$ ($\Delta{=}{+}0.153$) because structural signals continue to benefit from additional pairwise evidence while LP does not.
193	For sample selection, additional samples increase the oracle upper bound---more draws raise the probability of a high-quality candidate---but signal-based selection remains flat because the selector's inability to identify the best candidate is a granularity limitation, not a sample size limitation.
194	The practical implication is that $N{=}8$ captures most of the achievable quality estimation benefit; further scaling widens rather than closes the correlation-selection gap absent stronger per-sample selectors.
195	
196	\section{Bootstrap CI for Signal Comparisons}\label{app:bootstrap_signal}
197	
198	Table~\ref{tab:bootstrap_signal_ci} reports bootstrap confidence intervals for individual signal $\rho$ and pairwise $\Delta\rho$ on SciERC NER ($N{=}8$, $n{=}529$, $B{=}10{,}000$, seed${=}42$).
199	The SJ--LP gap ($\Delta\rho{=}{+}0.155$) is clearly significant, confirming the structural advantage reported in \S\ref{sec:qe_results}.
200	The VC--SJ gap ($\Delta\rho{=}{+}0.019$) is not significant, indicating that these two structural signals perform comparably.
201	The SJ--EM gap ($\Delta\rho{=}{+}0.065$) is borderline significant, with the CI lower bound at $+0.003$ and upper bound at $+0.130$.

236	\section{Diversity and Selection Analysis}\label{app:diversity}
237	
238	\paragraph{Output diversity quantification.}
239	Table~\ref{tab:diversity} summarizes output diversity across configurations.
240	On SciERC, output diversity is substantial: Qwen produces 43\% unique outputs per instance (mean 3.5 distinct out of $N{=}8$), with per-sample F1 standard deviation of 0.144 and mean pairwise SJ of 0.703.
241	LLaMA shows similar diversity (39\% unique, F1 std 0.11).
242	On CoNLL, diversity collapses: 57\% (Qwen) to 88\% (LLaMA) of instances produce identical outputs across all $N$ samples, reflecting a ceiling effect where greedy F1 already exceeds 0.90.
243	WNUT-17 shows 55\% identical outputs, driven by the 52\% zero-F1 rate.
244	
245	\begin{table}[t]
246	\centering
247	\small
248	\caption{Output diversity across configurations ($N{=}8$, $T{=}1.0$). ``Identical'' = fraction of instances with all $N$ outputs identical. ``Unique'' = mean fraction of distinct outputs per instance.}
249	\label{tab:diversity}
250	\begin{tabular}{lcc}
251	\toprule
252	Config & Identical & Unique \\
253	\midrule
254	Qwen/SciERC & 11\% & 43\% \\
255	LLaMA/SciERC & 18\% & 39\% \\
256	Qwen/CoNLL & 57\% & --- \\
257	LLaMA/CoNLL & 88\% & --- \\
258	WNUT-17 & 55\% & --- \\
259	\bottomrule
260	\end{tabular}
261	\end{table}
262	
263	\paragraph{Oracle-improvable fraction.}
264	Across SciERC configurations, 39--42\% of instances contain at least one sample with F1 exceeding greedy: Qwen NER 42.0\% (222/529, mean headroom $+$31.9~pp among improvable instances), Qwen RE 41.3\% (164/397, $+$45.3~pp), LLaMA NER 39.3\% (208/529, $+$33.7~pp).
265	On CoNLL, only 4--9\% of instances are improvable, confirming the ceiling effect.
266	
267	\paragraph{Diversity-stratified selection gap.}
268	On the 44\% of Qwen SciERC instances with high diversity (${\geq}4$ unique outputs among 8 samples, $n{=}232$), oracle headroom reaches $+$16.3~pp, yet all consistency-based signals (SJ, FK, EM, VC) select at or below greedy, and LP---the only per-sample signal---achieves merely $+$0.8~pp.
269	LLaMA replicates this pattern: on its high-diversity subset ($n{=}186$), oracle headroom reaches $+$20.1~pp while consistency signals remain at or below greedy and LP captures only $+$2.0~pp.
270	This rules out insufficient diversity as an explanation for the correlation-selection gap.
271	
272	\paragraph{TOST equivalence testing.}
273	The primary evidence against selection gains comes from effect sizes and confidence intervals: Cohen's $d{=}0.038$ for the best signal (NER LP) and bootstrap 95\% CI [$-$3.0, $+$4.2]~pp spanning zero.
274	Table~\ref{tab:tost} supplements this with two one-sided tests (TOST), testing whether each signal's selection F1 is equivalent to greedy within a bound $\delta$.
275	The $\delta$ values are anchored to oracle headroom (13--18~pp): $\delta{=}5$~pp corresponds to forgoing 28--38\% of the available headroom, $\delta{=}3$~pp forgoing 17--23\%, and $\delta{=}2$~pp less than 15\%.
276	The breakpoint $\delta^*$---the minimum equivalence margin at which each signal passes TOST---ranges from approximately 1.3~pp (SJ, ${\sim}$10\% of oracle headroom) to 3.4~pp (FK, ${\sim}$26\%) on NER, and from approximately 2.9~pp (LP, ${\sim}$16\%) to 6.5~pp (VC, ${\sim}$36\%) on RE.
277	On NER ($n{=}529$), at $\delta{=}{\pm}2$~pp, SJ and LP are confirmed equivalent to greedy (TOST $p{<}0.05$); at $\delta{=}{\pm}3$~pp, four of five signals pass; at $\delta{=}{\pm}5$~pp, all five pass.
278	On RE ($n{=}397$, preliminary), EM and VC fail equivalence even at $\delta{=}{\pm}5$~pp due to systematic underperformance ($\Delta{=}{-}3.2$~pp and $-4.5$~pp respectively).
279	The effect sizes are negligible ($d{=}0.038$) and confidence intervals span zero, with TOST confirming equivalence at practically meaningful margins.
280	
281	\begin{table}[t]
282	\centering
283	\small
284	\caption{TOST equivalence testing: signals vs.\ greedy on SciERC ($N{=}8$, $T{=}1.0$). \checkmark: equivalent at $p{<}0.05$; \texttimes: not equivalent. $\Delta$: mean F1 difference (signal $-$ greedy).}
285	\label{tab:tost}
286	\begin{tabular}{l cccc cccc}
287	\toprule
288	& \multicolumn{4}{c}{\textbf{NER}} & \multicolumn{4}{c}{\textbf{RE}} \\
289	\cmidrule(lr){2-5} \cmidrule(lr){6-9}
290	Signal & $\Delta$ & $\pm$2 & $\pm$3 & $\pm$5 & $\Delta$ & $\pm$2 & $\pm$3 & $\pm$5 \\
291	\midrule
292	LP & +0.6 & \checkmark & \checkmark & \checkmark & $-$0.9 & \texttimes & \checkmark & \checkmark \\
293	SJ & +0.1 & \checkmark & \checkmark & \checkmark & $-$1.8 & \texttimes & \texttimes & \checkmark \\
294	FK & $-$0.5 & \texttimes & \checkmark & \checkmark & $-$1.6 & \texttimes & \texttimes & \checkmark \\
295	EM & $-$0.8 & \texttimes & \checkmark & \checkmark & $-$3.2 & \texttimes & \texttimes & \texttimes \\
296	VC & $-$1.0 & \texttimes & \texttimes & \checkmark & $-$4.5 & \texttimes & \texttimes & \texttimes \\
297	\bottomrule
298	\end{tabular}
299	\end{table}
300	
301	\section{Multi-Seed Stability}\label{app:multiseed}
302	
303	Table~\ref{tab:multiseed} reports quality estimation metrics across three random seeds for both model families on SciERC NER ($N{=}8$, $T{=}1.0$).
304	The signal ranking VC${>}$SJ${>}$EM${>}$FK${>}$LP is identical across all six model-seed combinations, with $\sigma{\leq}0.025$ for all signals, confirming that our findings are robust to random seed variation.
305	LLaMA absolute values are systematically lower than Qwen, consistent with the directionally-consistent-but-magnitude-dependent characterization in the main text.
306	
307	\begin{table}[t]
308	\centering
309	\small
310	\caption{Multi-seed quality estimation on SciERC NER ($N{=}8$, $T{=}1.0$, seeds: 42, 123, 456). Top: Spearman $\rho$; bottom: AUROC. Signal ranking VC${>}$SJ${>}$EM${>}$FK${>}$LP is identical across all six model-seed combinations ($\sigma{\leq}0.025$).}
311	\label{tab:multiseed}

326	
327	\section{Training Configuration}\label{app:training}
328	
329	Both models are fine-tuned with identical hyperparameters (Table~\ref{tab:training}) to ensure comparisons across model families reflect model differences rather than training confounds.
330	
331	\begin{table}[t]
332	\centering
333	\small
334	\caption{Training configuration. Both Qwen3-8B and LLaMA-3.1-8B-Instruct use identical settings unless noted.}
335	\label{tab:training}
336	\begin{tabular}{ll}
337	\toprule
338	Parameter & Value \\
339	\midrule
340	LoRA rank / $\alpha$ & 32 / 64 \\
341	LoRA target & All linear modules \\
342	LoRA dropout & 0 \\
343	Optimizer & AdamW \\
344	Learning rate & 2e-4, cosine schedule \\
345	Warmup ratio & 0.1 \\
346	Effective batch size & 16 (2 $\times$ grad.\ accum.\ 8) \\
347	Epochs & 5 \\
348	Max sequence length & 2048 \\
349	Precision & bf16 \\
350	Gradient checkpointing & Enabled \\
351	Max gradient norm & 1.0 \\
352	Training seed & 42 \\
353	Framework & LLaMA Factory \\
354	\midrule
355	\multicolumn{2}{l}{\textit{Dataset-specific}} \\
356	SciERC split & 1861 / 275 / 551 \\
357	CoNLL / WNUT & +\,load\_best\_model\_at\_end, \\
358	& eval\_steps${=}$200, save\_limit${=}$5 \\
359	\bottomrule
360	\end{tabular}
361	\end{table}
362	
363	\paragraph{Prompt template.}
364	For NER, we use a structured prompt:
365	\begin{quote}
366	\small
367	\texttt{System:} You are an expert in Named Entity Recognition.\\
368	\texttt{User:} Extract all named entities from the following text. Output as JSON.\\
369	Text: \texttt{\{input\_text\}}\\
370	Entity types: \texttt{\{schema\_types\}}
371	\end{quote}
372	RE follows the same structure with ``Relation Extraction'' and relation type lists substituted accordingly.
373	
374	\paragraph{Constrained decoding.}
375	Inference uses vLLM with XGrammar-based JSON schema enforcement \cite{willard2023guided}, which constrains outputs to valid entity/relation structures matching the target schema.
376	This eliminates format errors but may compress per-sample diversity by restricting the output space.\footnote{SciERC training used \texttt{enable\_thinking=True} (LLaMA Factory 0.9.4 default), injecting empty \texttt{&lt;think>&lt;/think>} blocks (4--5 tokens) into each training response with loss computation; CoNLL and WNUT explicitly set \texttt{enable\_thinking=False}. Constrained decoding at inference time enforces JSON-only output, suppressing thinking tokens. See \S\ref{sec:setup} and Limitations for discussion of this confound.}
377	
378	\section{Log-Probability Score Compression}\label{app:logprob_ties}
379	
380	Under constrained decoding, log-probability scores are severely compressed within instances, limiting per-sample discrimination (Table~\ref{tab:logprob_ties}).
381	The median within-instance LP range is only 0.041, and 75\% of pairwise comparisons are effective ties ($|\Delta|{<}0.026$).
382	At $\varepsilon{=}0.05$, 60.3\% of instances have a within-sample range below the threshold, leaving LP unable to meaningfully distinguish candidates.
383	For comparison, SJ has only 12.5\% tied instances at the same threshold---SJ's advantage over LP for selection is one of score resolution, not information content.
384	
385	\begin{table}[t]
386	\centering
387	\small
388	\caption{Log-probability score compression on SciERC NER ($N{=}8$, $n{=}529$). ``Tied'' = fraction of instances where within-instance LP range ${<}\varepsilon$.}
389	\label{tab:logprob_ties}
390	\begin{tabular}{lc}
391	\toprule
392	$\varepsilon$ threshold & Tied instances (\%) \\
393	\midrule
394	0.01 & 1.1\% \\
395	0.05 & 60.3\% \\
396	0.1  & ${\sim}$84\% \\
397	0.5  & ${\sim}$99\% \\
398	\bottomrule
399	\end{tabular}
400	\end{table}
401	
402	\paragraph{Non-tied subset.}
403	Among instances with LP range ${\geq}0.05$ ($n{=}210$, 40\% of total), log-probability selection \textit{significantly} outperforms greedy ($+2.9$~pp F1, $p{=}0.01$), and within-instance $\rho(\text{LP}, \text{F1})$ rises from 0.08 (tied subset) to 0.39 (non-tied subset).
404	This confirms that LP carries useful per-sample quality information but is resolution-limited under constrained decoding---the signal exists but is compressed below the discrimination threshold for most instances.
405	
406	\section{RE Conditional Selection}\label{app:re_conditional_selection}
407	
408	Table~\ref{tab:re_cond_selection} extends the selection analysis to the RE non-zero F1 subset.
409	Of $n{=}397$ RE instances, 238 (60\%) have non-zero F1 and 159 (40\%) have zero F1.
410	All five signals underperform greedy on both subsets.
411	On the non-zero subset, selection performance is \textit{worse} than on the full set (e.g., VC: $-10.0$~pp on non-zero vs.\ $-4.5$~pp on full set), because greedy is already strong on non-zero instances (F1${=}0.674$) and signals actively select inferior samples.
412	Oracle headroom exists (0.757 vs.\ 0.674) but no signal exploits it, ruling out the 40\% zero-F1 floor effect as the explanation for RE selection failure.

19	\title{The Constrained Decoding Bottleneck: Why Confidence Signals\\Predict but Cannot Select in Information Extraction}
20	
21	\author{Anonymous}
22	
23	\date{}
24	
25	\begin{document}
26	\maketitle
27	
28	% ============================================================
29	\begin{abstract}
30	Test-time scaling via multi-sample generation improves LLM performance on reasoning tasks, but its effectiveness for structured information extraction (IE) remains unclear. We systematically evaluate five confidence signals---spanning log-probability, exact match, set similarity, token voting, and n-gram overlap---for selecting the best output among multiple candidates generated by fine-tuned LLMs on named entity recognition (NER) and relation extraction (RE). We identify a \emph{regime-specific selection bottleneck}: while these signals reliably estimate output quality (AUROC up to 0.75), they fail to select better candidates than greedy decoding under grammar-constrained generation ($\Delta$F1 $= +0.6$pp, $p = 0.39$). Diagnosis reveals that constrained decoding compresses log-probability variance---60\% of instances have within-sample log-prob range $< 0.05$---destroying the only per-sample ranking signal. A controlled ablation confirms the causal mechanism: removing grammar constraints recovers log-prob variance by 10$\times$ and enables significant selection gains (NER $+5.0$pp, $p = 0.008$). The signal ranking itself reverses: log-prob shifts from worst under constrained decoding ($\rho = 0.21$) to best under free-form generation ($\rho = 0.42$). A supervised DeBERTa verifier achieves only $+3.6$pp under constrained decoding, below the zero-cost free-form log-prob gain, confirming that the bottleneck lies in the decoding regime, not the scoring function. Our results provide the first controlled evidence that constrained decoding creates a fundamental selection barrier for structured IE, and suggest relaxed decoding with post-hoc validation as a practical mitigation path.
31	\end{abstract}
32	
33	% ============================================================
34	\section{Introduction}
35	\label{sec:intro}
36	
37	Multi-sample generation at test time has emerged as a powerful strategy for improving LLM outputs. Self-consistency \citep{wang2023selfconsistency} selects answers by majority vote, MBR decoding \citep{eikema2022sampling} selects translations by consensus similarity, and best-of-N sampling with reward models \citep{cobbe2021training,lightman2023lets} selects reasoning chains by learned quality scores. These methods share a common template: generate $N$ candidates, score each, select the best.
38	
39	For structured information extraction---NER and RE tasks where outputs are \emph{sets} of entities or relation triples---this template faces a challenge that prior work has not systematically examined. Modern IE systems increasingly use constrained (grammar-guided) decoding \citep{willard2023efficient} to guarantee well-formed structured outputs, but the interaction between constrained decoding and candidate selection has not been studied. Meanwhile, the few studies on consistency-based confidence for IE \citep{nedellec2026consistency} rely on surface-level string matching and do not examine selection performance.
40	
41	We present a systematic evaluation of five confidence signals for structured IE quality estimation and selection, and discover a \textbf{regime-specific selection bottleneck} caused by constrained decoding. Our investigation proceeds in three stages:
42	
43	\textbf{(1) Quality estimation succeeds, selection fails.} We evaluate five signal families on SciERC and CoNLL-2003 across two model architectures (Qwen3-8B, LLaMA-3.1-8B). All structural signals achieve strong quality estimation (AUROC up to 0.75). Yet no signal significantly outperforms greedy decoding at best-of-$N$ selection (NER: $+0.6$pp, $p = 0.39$; RE: greedy dominates). We call this the \emph{correlation--selection gap}.
44	
45	\textbf{(2) Constrained decoding compresses log-probabilities.} We diagnose the mechanism: grammar-guided decoding masks invalid tokens at each step, collapsing the log-probability distribution. Across 529 NER instances, 60\% have within-sample log-prob range below 0.05---candidates are indistinguishable to the only per-sample ranking signal. The four structural signals (SJ, FK, EM, voting) are instance-level aggregates that cannot rank candidates by construction.
46	
47	\textbf{(3) Controlled ablation confirms the causal mechanism.} Removing grammar constraints while keeping all other variables fixed (same model, data, $N$, temperature) recovers log-prob variance by 10$\times$ and enables statistically significant selection: NER $+5.0$pp ($p = 0.008$), RE $+3.2$pp ($p = 0.032$). The signal ranking reverses entirely---log-prob goes from worst ($\rho = 0.21$) to best ($\rho = 0.42$), while structural signals remain stable.
48	
49	A supervised DeBERTa verifier trained with labeled data achieves only $+3.6$pp under constrained decoding, below the zero-cost free-form log-prob gain of $+5.0$pp. This confirms that the bottleneck is the decoding regime, not the quality of the scoring function.
50	
51	Our contributions are: (1) the first controlled identification and verification of constrained decoding as a causal bottleneck for test-time selection in structured IE; (2) a systematic five-signal evaluation framework with cross-model, cross-dataset validation; and (3) evidence that relaxed decoding is a more effective mitigation than stronger scoring functions.
52	
53	% ============================================================
54	\section{Related Work}
55	\label{sec:related}
56	
57	\paragraph{Test-Time Scaling and Sample Selection.}
58	Self-consistency \citep{wang2023selfconsistency} selects answers by majority vote over chain-of-thought samples. Extensions include confidence-weighted voting \citep[CISC;][]{cisc2025}, KL-divergence-based reward-free selection \citep[Self-Certainty;][]{selfcertainty2026}, reliability-aware adaptive sampling \citep[ReASC;][]{reasc2026}, ranked voting \citep{rankedvoting2025}, and atomic sub-claim consistency \citep{atomicsc2024}. These methods target discrete-answer reasoning tasks. We address set-structured IE outputs where majority vote is undefined and candidate comparison requires structural metrics.
59	
60	\paragraph{Uncertainty Quantification for LLMs.}
61	\citet{uqsurvey2025} and \citet{uqcalibsurvey2025} provide comprehensive surveys. Semantic entropy \citep{kuhn2023semantic} clusters generations by meaning; semantic entropy probes \citep{seprobes2024} and latent self-consistency \citep{latentsc2025} reduce cost via embedding spaces. SelfCheckGPT \citep{manakul2023selfcheckgpt} detects hallucinations via sample consistency. CoCoA \citep{cocoa2025} combines confidence and consistency for MBR-style selection on QA and summarization. \citet{vashurin2025systematic} systematically evaluate four UQ methods but do not cover structured IE. Our work fills this gap by evaluating confidence signals specifically for structured outputs under constrained decoding.
62	
63	\paragraph{Confidence and Calibration.}
64	Calibration of neural models \citep{guo2017calibration}, selective prediction \citep{geifman2017selective,abstentionsurvey2025}, and LLM confidence estimation \citep{confestsurvey2024} are well-studied for classification and generation. For structured prediction, calibration methods exist for sequences \citep{kuleshov2015calibration} and NER \citep{jagannatha2022calibrating}. Token-level NER uncertainty has been studied via MC-dropout \citep{gal2016dropout,xiao2019quantifying,shelmanov2021certain} and ensembles \citep{lakshminarayanan2017simple,he2020towards}. We evaluate \emph{sample-level} confidence for multi-candidate selection, a distinct problem from token-level uncertainty.
65	
66	\paragraph{Consistency for Structured IE.}
67	Most closely related, \citet{nedellec2026consistency} study consistency--accuracy correlation in hard-prompted LLMs for NER and RE. They use surface-level string matching, which they acknowledge ``overlooks semantically consistent outputs.'' Our Set Jaccard metric directly addresses this limitation. Unlike their work, we (a) study best-of-$N$ selection beyond correlation, (b) use fine-tuned models with constrained decoding, (c) evaluate five signal families, and (d) provide a controlled free-form ablation identifying the causal bottleneck.
68	
69	\paragraph{Constrained Decoding and Structured Generation.}
70	Grammar-guided generation \citep{willard2023efficient} guarantees syntactically valid outputs. While extensively used in IE pipelines, its effect on output diversity and downstream selection has not been studied. We provide the first evidence that grammar masking compresses log-probability variance and creates a selection bottleneck.
71	
72	\paragraph{Verifiers and Reward Models.}
73	Outcome reward models \citep{cobbe2021training} and process reward models \citep{lightman2023lets} enable best-of-$N$ selection for reasoning tasks. Test-time compute scaling theory \citep{snell2024scaling,brown2024large} analyzes when additional samples help. We complement this line by showing that in constrained-decoding IE, the bottleneck is candidate diversity rather than verifier quality.
74	
75	% ============================================================
76	\section{Method}
77	\label{sec:method}
78	
79	\subsection{Problem Setting}
80	\label{sec:problem}
81	
82	Given an input $x$ (e.g., a sentence), a fine-tuned LLM generates $N$ candidate outputs $\{y_1, \ldots, y_N\}$ via sampling at temperature $T$. Each $y_i$ is a structured object: a set of entities for NER or a set of relation triples for RE. The \textbf{quality estimation} problem is to predict the quality $q(y_i, y^*)$ of each candidate relative to ground truth $y^*$ without access to $y^*$. The \textbf{selection} problem is to choose $\hat{y} = \arg\max_i s(y_i)$ using a scoring function $s$ to maximize $q(\hat{y}, y^*)$.
83	
84	We evaluate quality $q$ as micro-F1 between the candidate's entity/relation set and the gold set, using type-grouped Hungarian matching to handle set alignment.
85	
86	\subsection{Confidence Signals}
87	\label{sec:signals}
88	
89	We evaluate five signals spanning four families:
90	
91	\paragraph{Log-probability (LogProb).}
92	The normalized sequence log-probability:
93	\begin{equation}
94	s_{\text{lp}}(y_i) = \frac{1}{|y_i|} \sum_{t=1}^{|y_i|} \log p_\theta(y_i^{(t)} | y_i^{(<t)}, x)
95	\end{equation}
96	This is the \emph{only} signal that produces a per-sample score enabling direct candidate ranking.
97	
98	\paragraph{Set Jaccard Similarity (SJ).}
99	The average pairwise Jaccard similarity between entity/relation sets:
100	\begin{equation}

```text
100	\begin{equation}
101	s_{\text{sj}}(x) = \frac{1}{\binom{N}{2}} \sum_{i < j} \frac{|S_i \cap S_j|}{|S_i \cup S_j|}
102	\end{equation}
103	where $S_i$ is the entity set extracted from $y_i$. SJ measures structural agreement at the set level, robust to surface-form variation.
104	
105	\paragraph{Fleiss' Kappa (FK).}
106	Inter-rater agreement treating the $N$ samples as annotators and entity spans as items. Measures consistency at the entity level.
107	
108	\paragraph{Exact Match Rate (EM).}
109	The fraction of sample pairs that produce identical output strings:
110	\begin{equation}
111	s_{\text{em}}(x) = \frac{1}{\binom{N}{2}} \sum_{i < j} \mathbb{1}[y_i = y_j]
112	\end{equation}
113	
114	\paragraph{Voting Confidence (VotConf).}
115	Entity-level voting: for each unique entity across $N$ samples, the fraction of samples that produce it. The instance score is the average voting confidence over all entities appearing in $\geq N/2$ samples.
116	
117	\paragraph{Instance-level vs.\ per-sample distinction.}
118	A critical observation: SJ, FK, EM, and VotConf produce a \emph{single score per instance} $x$---they measure agreement across the $N$ candidates but do not differentiate among them. Only LogProb produces a per-sample score $s(y_i)$. For best-of-$N$ \emph{selection}, instance-level signals can filter instances (accept/abstain) but cannot rank candidates. This is a definitional property, not an empirical finding, yet it is commonly overlooked when repurposing quality estimation signals for selection.
119	
120	For selection with instance-level signals, we use the consensus-proximity strategy: select the candidate closest to the consensus (highest average similarity to other candidates), analogous to MBR decoding \citep{eikema2022sampling}.
121	
122	\subsection{Evaluation Metrics}
123	\label{sec:metrics}
124	
125	\paragraph{Quality estimation.} Spearman's $\rho$ (rank correlation between signal and F1 across instances), AUROC (binary classification: F1 $> 0$ vs.\ F1 $= 0$), and Expected Calibration Error (ECE).
126	
127	\paragraph{Selection.} Best-of-$N$ F1: the F1 of the selected candidate. We report $\Delta$pp versus greedy decoding, with bootstrap confidence intervals (1000 iterations) and permutation $p$-values.
128	
129	% ============================================================
130	\section{Experimental Setup}
131	\label{sec:setup}
132	
133	\paragraph{Models.} Qwen3-8B \citep{qwen2024} and LLaMA-3.1-8B-Instruct \citep{llama31}, both fine-tuned on the respective training sets.
134	
135	\paragraph{Datasets.} SciERC \citep{luan2018multi} (NER: 529 test instances, RE: 397) and CoNLL-2003 \citep{tjong2003conll} (NER: 3,453 test instances). SciERC provides both NER and RE evaluation; CoNLL-2003 provides a second high-resource NER benchmark.
136	
137	\paragraph{Decoding.}
138	\emph{Constrained}: Outlines grammar-guided decoding \citep{willard2023efficient} with JSON schema constraints, $N = 8$ samples at $T = 1.0$.
139	\emph{Free-form}: identical settings without grammar constraints; outputs parsed post-hoc (90\% NER parse rate, 88\% RE).
140	
141	\paragraph{Baselines.} Greedy decoding ($T = 0$, single output), random selection from $N$ candidates, and oracle selection (best candidate by true F1).
142	
143	% ============================================================
144	\section{Results}
145	\label{sec:results}
146	
147	\subsection{Quality Estimation Under Constrained Decoding}
148	\label{sec:qe}
149	
150	Table~\ref{tab:qe_scierc} presents quality estimation results on SciERC with Qwen3-8B under constrained decoding. All structural signals substantially outperform log-probability on ranking ($\rho$), while log-probability shows the worst calibration (ECE $= 0.706$). On binary discrimination (AUROC), exact match leads for NER (0.749) and RE (0.706).

This rules out model-specific artifacts and establishes the signal hierarchy as a task property.

197	\subsection{The Correlation--Selection Gap}
198	\label{sec:gap}
199	
200	Despite strong quality estimation, no signal achieves significant selection gains under constrained decoding (Table~\ref{tab:selection_constrained}). On NER, the best signal (LogProb) achieves $+0.6$pp over greedy, with bootstrap $p = 0.39$ and Cohen's $d = 0.038$ (negligible effect). On RE, greedy decoding outperforms all signals. The oracle ceiling reveals substantial headroom (NER: $+13.2$pp, RE: $+18.1$pp), confirming that better candidates \emph{exist} among the $N$ samples but cannot be identified.
201

203	\centering
204	\caption{Best-of-$N$ selection F1 under constrained decoding (SciERC, Qwen3-8B). No signal significantly outperforms greedy. Oracle headroom confirms selection failure, not candidate quality.}
205	\label{tab:selection_constrained}

226	
227	\subsection{Diagnosing the Bottleneck: Log-Prob Compression}
228	\label{sec:diagnosis}
229	
230	Why does log-probability---the only per-sample signal---fail at selection? Under constrained decoding, grammar masking sets invalid tokens' logits to $-\infty$ at each decode step. For JSON-schema IE outputs, structural tokens (\texttt{\{}, \texttt{"entities"}, \texttt{:}, etc.) are often the \emph{only} valid token at a given step, contributing $\log p \approx 0$. Only entity name and type tokens offer genuine choice. Consequently, the sequence-level log-probabilities of the $N$ candidates collapse to a narrow range.
231	
232	Quantitatively, across 529 NER instances, the median within-sample log-prob range (max $-$ min over $N = 8$ candidates) is just 0.031, and 60\% of instances have range $< 0.05$. The candidates are effectively indistinguishable by log-prob, making ranking impossible regardless of the underlying quality variation.
233	
234	% ============================================================
235	\subsection{Controlled Ablation: Free-Form Decoding}
236	\label{sec:freeform}
237	
238	To test whether constrained decoding \emph{causes} the selection failure, we conduct a controlled ablation: identical model, data, $N$, and temperature, but without grammar constraints. Outputs are parsed post-hoc; 90\% yield valid NER parses ($n = 476$) and 88\% yield valid RE parses ($n = 350$).
239	
240	\paragraph{Log-prob variance recovery.}
241	Table~\ref{tab:freeform_main} shows the core result. Removing grammar constraints increases the median within-sample log-prob range by $10.3\times$ (NER: $0.031 \to 0.32$; Wilcoxon $p = 1.2 \times 10^{-45}$). The fraction of instances with range $< 0.05$ drops from 60\% to 8\%.
242	
243	\paragraph{Selection performance recovery.}
244	With variance restored, log-prob selection achieves $+5.0$pp over greedy on NER ($p = 0.008$, Cohen's $d = 0.31$, 95\% CI [$+1.8$, $+8.2$]pp) and $+3.2$pp on RE ($p = 0.032$). This captures 33.8\% of the oracle headroom on NER and 16.5\% on RE.
245

249	\label{tab:freeform_main}

266	
267	\paragraph{Signal ranking reversal.}
268	Table~\ref{tab:freeform_signals} compares all five signals under free-form decoding. The most striking finding is the complete reversal of log-prob's ranking position:
269	\begin{itemize}
270	\item \textbf{Constrained}: LogProb $\rho = 0.205$ (worst) $\ll$ SJ $\rho = 0.360$ $<$ VotConf $\rho = 0.379$ (best)
271	\item \textbf{Free-form}: LogProb $\rho = 0.420$ (best) $>$ VotConf $\rho = 0.390$ $>$ SJ $\rho = 0.370$
272	\end{itemize}
273	The same signal goes from worst to best simply by changing the decoding regime. Structural signals (SJ, VotConf) remain stable across regimes, confirming they are unaffected by log-prob compression---and confirming that the compression specifically targets log-prob.

296	\subsection{Supervised Verifier Baseline}
297	\label{sec:verifier}
298	
299	To establish an upper bound on selection under constrained decoding, we train a supervised DeBERTa-v3-large verifier (304M parameters) via 5-fold cross-validation. The verifier takes (sentence, model output) pairs and predicts F1 scores using MSE loss over 5 epochs.
300	
301	Table~\ref{tab:verifier} shows that the verifier substantially outperforms unsupervised signals on quality estimation (NER: $\rho = 0.52$ vs.\ $0.379$; AUROC $= 0.82$ vs.\ $0.749$), confirming the gap is closable with supervision. However, its selection gain under constrained decoding is modest: $+3.6$pp on NER, with 27.3\% headroom capture.

326	\paragraph{Free-form log-prob outperforms the supervised verifier.}
327	Table~\ref{tab:hierarchy} presents the complete gap-closure hierarchy. The zero-cost free-form log-prob ($+5.0$pp, 33.8\% headroom) exceeds the supervised verifier operating on constrained outputs ($+3.6$pp, 27.3\% headroom). This is the paper's strongest evidence that the bottleneck lies in candidate diversity (determined by the decoding regime), not in the quality of the scoring function. Even a trained verifier with access to labeled data cannot overcome the homogeneity of constrained-decoding candidates.

350	% ============================================================
351	\section{Discussion}
352	\label{sec:discussion}
353	
354	\paragraph{Why constrained decoding compresses log-prob.}
355	Grammar-guided decoding applies a token-level mask at each generation step, setting logits of grammar-invalid tokens to $-\infty$. For JSON-schema IE, the majority of decode steps have a single valid continuation (structural tokens like brackets, colons, and key names). Only a small fraction of steps---those generating entity names and types---offer genuine vocabulary choice. The sequence-level log-prob, averaged over all steps, is dominated by the near-deterministic structural steps, washing out the quality-relevant variation in entity steps.
356	
357	\paragraph{Why structural signals are unaffected.}
358	SJ, FK, EM, and VotConf operate on the \emph{parsed output} (entity sets), not on token-level probabilities. They are invariant to the decoding regime because they compare extracted structures, not generation processes. Their stability across constrained and free-form regimes (SJ: $0.360 \to 0.370$) serves as a natural control: the same model on the same data produces similar structural agreement regardless of how tokens are selected.
359	
360	\paragraph{When would consistency-based selection help?}
361	Our negative result for structural signals is specific to the best-of-$N$ selection problem under fine-tuned models. Consistency-based confidence may outperform log-prob when: (a) the model is poorly calibrated, as in few-shot or in-context learning without fine-tuning; (b) log-probabilities are unavailable, as with closed-source APIs; (c) ensemble or mixture-of-expert settings where token-level scores are not comparable across components; or (d) tasks where the model is systematically overconfident and consistency provides an independent quality signal.
362	
363	\paragraph{Practical implications.}
364	Our results suggest a concrete mitigation: generate candidates without grammar constraints, parse outputs post-hoc, and select among parseable candidates using log-prob. On our fine-tuned models, 90\% of free-form outputs are parseable, and the 10\% parse failure rate is a modest cost for $+5.0$pp selection gain. For deployment scenarios where 100\% parse rate is required, a hybrid approach---free-form generation with constrained fallback for unparseable outputs---may capture most of the benefit.
365	
366	\paragraph{Limitations.}
367	(1) Our experiments use two 8B-parameter models; larger models may exhibit different compression patterns. (2) We evaluate on two NER datasets and one RE dataset; informal-text domains (social media, clinical notes) are not covered. (3) The free-form parse rate of 90\% may be lower for less fine-tuned models. (4) We study sampling at $T = 1.0$; the interaction between temperature and constrained decoding deserves further investigation.
368	
369	% ============================================================
370	\section{Conclusion}
371	
372	We identified and experimentally verified a regime-specific selection bottleneck in structured information extraction: constrained (grammar-guided) decoding compresses log-probability scores, destroying the only per-sample ranking signal and preventing effective best-of-$N$ selection despite strong quality estimation performance (AUROC up to 0.75). A controlled free-form ablation confirmed the causal mechanism---removing grammar constraints recovered log-prob variance by $10\times$ and enabled significant NER selection gains of $+5.0$pp ($p = 0.008$). The signal ranking reversed entirely, with log-prob going from worst to best, providing direct evidence that constrained decoding is the cause. A supervised verifier achieved only $+3.6$pp under constrained decoding, below the zero-cost free-form log-prob gain, confirming the bottleneck lies in candidate diversity rather than scoring quality. These findings suggest that relaxed decoding with post-hoc validation is a more promising direction than stronger scoring functions for test-time scaling in structured IE.
373

566	% ============================================================
567	\appendix
568	\section{Out-of-Domain Robustness}
569	\label{app:ood}
570	
571	To test whether the constrained decoding bottleneck persists under domain shift, we evaluate SciERC-fine-tuned Qwen3-8B on WNUT-17 (zero-shot cross-domain NER, $n = 1{,}287$). Table~\ref{tab:ood} shows results.
572	
573	Performance drops substantially (greedy F1: $0.32$ vs.\ in-domain $0.64$), and 58\% of samples produce F1 $= 0$ due to schema mismatch. The bottleneck pattern persists: log-prob $\rho = -0.08$ (negative, indicating overconfidence on familiar patterns), while structural signals remain weakly positive (VotConf $\rho = 0.18$, SJ $\rho = 0.15$). Log-prob compression is more severe (range median $= 0.02$), consistent with the constrained decoding mechanism. This rules out the alternative hypothesis that the bottleneck is specific to in-domain settings.
574	
575	\begin{table}[h]
576	\centering
577	\caption{OOD signal robustness (SciERC $\to$ WNUT-17, Qwen3-8B, constrained, $N\!=\!8$). Bottleneck pattern persists: log-prob negative, structural signals weakly positive.}
578	\label{tab:ood}
579	\small
580	\begin{tabular}{l cc}
581	\toprule
582	Signal & $\rho$ & AUROC \\
583	\midrule
584	VotConf & \bmark{0.180} & \bmark{0.600} \\
585	SJ      & 0.150 & 0.580 \\
586	FK      & 0.120 & 0.560 \\
587	EM      & 0.110 & 0.570 \\
588	LogProb & $-$0.080 & 0.480 \\
589	\midrule
590	\multicolumn{3}{l}{\small Greedy F1: 0.32, Oracle F1: 0.52} \\
591	\multicolumn{3}{l}{\small 58\% samples F1 $= 0$, logprob range median $= 0.02$} \\
592	\bottomrule
593	\end{tabular}
594	\end{table}

72	
73	1. **Score compression hypothesis NOT confirmed**: Free-form LP variance is virtually identical to constrained (ratio 0.97x for std, 0.96x for range). Removing constrained decoding did NOT meaningfully increase logprob spread. The tied fraction decreased by only 2.0pp (59.7% → 57.7%). This suggests logprob compression is intrinsic to the model/task, not an artifact of constrained decoding.
74	
75	2. **QE signals uniformly improve under free-form** (except RE voting_conf): All 5 signals show higher ρ in NER (+0.0077 to +0.0532). In RE, 4 of 5 signals improve (+0.0024 to +0.0393), with only VC declining (-0.0263). FK shows the largest consistent gains (NER +0.0532, RE +0.0393).
76	
77	3. **LP selection shows divergent behavior by subtask**: NER selection gain shrinks from +0.0059 (constrained) to +0.0018 (free-form). RE selection flips from negative (-0.0068) to strongly positive (+0.0169). Free-form enables effective LP-based selection for RE where constrained decoding failed.
78	
79	4. **Near-perfect parse rate (99.95%)**: Only 2 of 4408 samples failed parsing, and 549/551 instances (99.6%) have all 8 samples valid. Free-form decoding with Qwen on SciERC is highly reliable — constrained decoding is not needed for format compliance.
80	
81	5. **Greedy F1 is comparable across conditions**: NER greedy F1 differs by only 0.0022 (0.6182 vs 0.6204) and RE by 0.0033 (0.2913 vs 0.2880), confirming the comparison is apples-to-apples on a well-matched task difficulty.
82	
```

<details><summary>↩ Tool result · `toolu_01QWYhsDWdXZSEjNsZqMsVbE`</summary>

```text
1	\begin{abstract}
2	Test-time scaling through multi-sample generation promises quality improvements for LLM-based information extraction, but which confidence signals best serve quality estimation versus sample selection remains unclear.
3	We systematically compare five confidence signals from three families---structural (entity voting, Soft Jaccard), surface (exact match, Fleiss' $\kappa$), and model-internal (log-probability)---across NER and relation extraction on three datasets (SciERC, CoNLL-2003, WNUT-17) with two model families (Qwen3-8B, LLaMA-3.1-8B).
4	We find that entity voting achieves $\rho{=}0.50$ and AUROC${=}0.73$ for quality estimation, with structural signals dominating on complex schemas while surface signals lead on simpler tasks; however, this strong correlation does not translate into selection gains---no signal surpasses greedy decoding on NER best-of-$N$, while only log-probability helps on relation extraction (+2.3~pp).
5	We term this disconnect the \textit{correlation-selection gap}: instance-level consistency signals predict which inputs the model handles reliably, not which output is best among $N$ candidates.
6	Signal rankings further depend on task difficulty---exact match dominates easy tasks, structural signals dominate hard tasks, and all signals converge when model capability is insufficient---establishing that signal choice should be guided by task difficulty and intended application.
7	These results reframe consistency signals as quality estimators rather than output selectors for structured IE.
8	\end{abstract}
9	
```

<details><summary>↩ Tool result · `toolu_01QijBShFEY2PptySfN1UEmF`</summary>

```text
1	\section{Introduction}\label{sec:introduction}
2	
3	Self-consistency---sampling multiple outputs and aggregating them---has become a powerful strategy for improving language model reasoning.
4	On discrete-answer tasks such as arithmetic and commonsense QA, majority voting over chain-of-thought samples yields large gains: +18 percentage points on GSM8K \cite{wang2023selfconsistency}.
5	More broadly, test-time compute scaling through repeated sampling and selection has emerged as a complementary axis to model scaling, offering substantial quality improvements at inference time \cite{snell2024scaling, brown2024monkeys}.
6	These results rest on a shared assumption: that among $N$ stochastic samples, a selection mechanism can identify better outputs.
7	For tasks with a single correct answer, this assumption holds naturally---the majority vote is the selector.
8	But information extraction (IE) poses a fundamentally different challenge.
9	The output is not a single token or number; it is a \textit{set} of entities or relations with internal dependencies, where two samples may extract overlapping but non-identical structures.
10	There is no obvious majority to vote on, partial agreement is the norm rather than the exception, and the space of valid outputs is combinatorially large.
11	Can self-consistency still provide useful signal in this structured setting?
12	
13	Prior work on confidence estimation for LLM-based IE has conflated two distinct objectives.
14	\textit{Quality estimation} asks which inputs the model handles reliably---a ranking over instances.
15	\textit{Sample selection} asks which of $N$ candidate outputs is best for a given input---a ranking over samples.
16	These require fundamentally different signal properties: the former benefits from instance-level aggregation across all $N$ samples, while the latter demands per-sample discrimination.
17	Existing evaluations rarely separate the two.
18	Self-consistency methods designed for discrete answers \cite{wang2023selfconsistency, chen2024usc} assume outputs can be grouped by equivalence and counted, an assumption that breaks when outputs are entity sets with partial overlap.
19	Weighted variants that incorporate model confidence \cite{taubenfeld2025cisc, kang2025selfcertainty} have shown promise for best-of-$N$ selection on reasoning tasks, but their applicability to structured prediction remains untested.
20	The most directly relevant study, \citet{nedellec2026consistency}, examines consistency--accuracy correlations for LLM-based entity and relation extraction but relies on surface-level matching, a limitation the authors themselves acknowledge: their approach ``overlooks semantically consistent outputs'' that differ only in surface form.
21	No existing work systematically compares confidence signals across the structural, surface, and model-internal families for generative IE, nor disentangles quality estimation from sample selection in this setting.
22	
23	Our central finding is that consistency-based signals are effective instance-level quality estimators but poor sample selectors for structured IE.
24	Consistency signals aggregate over $N$ samples to produce a single score per input, making them well-suited to predict input difficulty---instances where the model consistently extracts similar structures are indeed easier.
25	Entity voting confidence, our best signal, achieves Spearman $\rho{=}0.50$ and AUROC${=}0.73$ on SciERC NER, a correlation strong enough to be practically useful for quality-aware routing.
26	Yet this correlation does not translate into selection gains: on NER best-of-$N$, no signal surpasses greedy decoding despite 16 percentage points of oracle headroom.
27	We term this the \textbf{correlation-selection gap} (Figure~\ref{fig:overview}).
28	The gap arises because instance-level signals cannot discriminate among $N$ candidates that share similar consistency profiles---they predict \textit{which inputs} are hard, not \textit{which output} is best.
29	Only log-probability, the sole per-sample signal in our comparison, achieves selection gains, and only on relation extraction (+2.3~pp over greedy), where per-sample variance is higher.
30	We systematically evaluate five confidence signals from three families---structural (entity voting, Soft Jaccard), surface (exact match, Fleiss' $\kappa$), and model-internal (log-probability)---across NER and RE on SciERC \cite{luan2018scierc}, CoNLL-2003 \cite{tjongkimsang2003conll}, and WNUT-17 \cite{derczynski2017wnut}, using Qwen3-8B and LLaMA-3.1-8B.
31	
32	Our contributions are as follows:
33	\begin{enumerate}
34	    \item \textbf{First systematic comparison of confidence signals for generative IE.}
35	    We evaluate five signals from three families on quality estimation for NER and RE.
36	    Entity voting achieves $\rho{=}0.50$ and AUROC${=}0.73$; structural signals outperform surface and model-internal alternatives on complex schemas (SciERC), while surface signals dominate on simpler tasks (CoNLL).
37	
38	    \item \textbf{Identification of the correlation-selection gap.}
39	    We demonstrate a fundamental disconnect between quality estimation and sample selection in structured IE.
40	    Despite strong instance-level correlations ($\rho$ up to 0.50), no consistency signal improves NER best-of-$N$ over greedy decoding; only log-probability helps on RE (+2.3~pp).
41	    This gap reflects the instance-level nature of consistency signals, which predict input difficulty rather than discriminating among individual samples.
42	
43	    \item \textbf{Task-complexity boundary conditions.}
44	    Signal rankings depend on task difficulty: exact match leads on easy tasks (CoNLL), Soft Jaccard dominates on hard tasks (SciERC), and all signals converge when model capability is insufficient (WNUT-17, where 54\% of instances receive zero F1).
45	    This establishes practical guidance for signal selection based on expected task difficulty.
46	\end{enumerate}
47	
48	The remainder of this paper is organized as follows.
49	\S\ref{sec:method} formalizes the problem and defines the five confidence signals.
50	\S\ref{sec:experiments} presents quality estimation results, the correlation-selection gap analysis, and robustness evaluations.
51	\S\ref{sec:related} situates our work in the broader landscape of self-consistency, uncertainty estimation, and LLM-based IE.
52	
```

_source line 65 · 2026-05-15T10:10:33.210Z_



I now have a comprehensive understanding of the paper across its multiple versions. There are two key versions: (1) the `docs/paper/` version (the actual submitted paper, which does NOT include the free-form ablation or verifier experiments), and (2) the `dream_reports/dream_20260515T092545/best/paper.tex` version (the aspirational "best" version that includes free-form ablation and DeBERTa verifier results). Critically, the actual free-form ablation experiment (`freeform_ablation_results.md`) **contradicts** the claims in the dream paper -- the log-prob compression was NOT relieved by removing constrained decoding.

Let me now write the review.

## R2 Review: Novelty and Related Work

### Summary (2-3 sentences)

This paper evaluates five confidence signals for best-of-N selection in LLM-based structured information extraction (NER, RE) under constrained decoding. The central finding is a "correlation-selection gap": signals that predict instance-level quality (AUROC up to 0.75) fail to select better candidates than greedy decoding, attributed to log-probability compression under grammar-guided generation. The paper provides a systematic five-signal comparison across three datasets and two model families, with the claimed contribution being identification of constrained decoding as the causal bottleneck for selection failure.

### Strengths

- **S1: Well-posed research question with practical importance.** The paper clearly articulates a distinction between quality estimation and sample selection that is genuinely underappreciated in the test-time scaling literature. The framing that strong QE does not imply strong selection is a useful conceptual contribution that practitioners deploying multi-sample IE pipelines will benefit from.

- **S2: Thorough experimental protocol with appropriate statistical rigor.** The use of bootstrap CIs, permutation tests, Cohen's d, TOST equivalence testing, multi-seed stability analysis, and cross-model validation (Qwen3-8B and LLaMA-3.1-8B) significantly exceeds what many empirical NLP papers provide. The 3-seed stability analysis showing sigma <= 0.025 is particularly reassuring.

- **S3: Comprehensive related work coverage.** The paper cites ~60 references spanning self-consistency, UQ, calibration, constrained decoding, verifiers, and structured IE. The positioning against Nedellec and Xia (2026) is explicit and honest about the three specific advances claimed. The inclusion of very recent work (CISC, Self-Certainty, ReASC, Latent SC, CoCoA, Vashurin 2025b) demonstrates thorough awareness of the current landscape.

- **S4: The instance-level vs. per-sample distinction is well-articulated.** Although the paper correctly notes this is definitional rather than empirical, stating it clearly and then focusing attention on the non-trivial questions (can consensus-proximity / MBR selection recover discrimination? can log-prob exploit the headroom?) is effective scientific writing.

- **S5: The MBR connection is insightful.** Framing consensus-proximity selection as MBR decoding and noting that utility-quality correlation holds across instances but not within them is a genuinely interesting observation, especially given MBR's success in MT. The citation of Eikema et al. (2025) on structure-conditional MBR degradation strengthens this point.

- **S6: Honest about limitations.** The paper flags the RE single-benchmark limitation repeatedly (labeled "preliminary"), acknowledges the Qwen3 thinking confound, and notes the free-form ablation as planned rather than completed (in the submitted version). This transparency is commendable.

### Weaknesses

- **W1: The core "constrained decoding bottleneck" claim has a serious evidentiary gap.** The submitted paper (`docs/paper/`) presents the constrained-decoding compression hypothesis as the explanation for selection failure but acknowledges the free-form ablation as a "[Planned experiment]." The dream/aspirational version claims 10x variance recovery and +5.0pp gains under free-form decoding, but the **actual experimental results** (`freeform_ablation_results.md`) show the opposite: free-form LP variance is virtually identical to constrained (ratio 0.97x for std, 1.01x for median range), and the tied fraction drops by only 2.0pp (59.7% to 57.7%). This means the causal mechanism claimed in the title and abstract -- that constrained decoding *causes* the log-prob compression -- is not supported by the evidence. The compression appears to be intrinsic to the model/task. This is a fundamental problem for the paper's primary contribution. If the authors cannot demonstrate that free-form generation recovers LP variance, the paper's narrative must be substantially revised from "constrained decoding bottleneck" to "log-prob compression is an inherent limitation of fine-tuned IE models."

- **W2: The novelty of the "correlation-selection gap" concept is limited.** The observation that instance-level metrics cannot rank candidates within an instance is, as the paper itself admits, "definitional" for 4/5 signals. The remaining empirical question -- whether LP can exploit oracle headroom -- reduces to asking whether LP scores have sufficient resolution under constrained decoding. Score compression under constrained generation is not surprising given the well-understood mechanics of grammar masking (most tokens are deterministic structural tokens). Willard and Louf (2023) describe the FSM-based token masking that makes many generation steps near-deterministic; the consequence for sequence-level score variance follows straightforwardly. The paper would benefit from acknowledging this more explicitly rather than presenting the compression observation as if it were unexpected.

- **W3: Positioning against Nedellec and Xia (2026) overstates the gap.** The four claimed advances are: (a) best-of-N selection beyond correlation, (b) fine-tuned + constrained decoding, (c) five signal families, (d) free-form ablation. Advance (a) is genuine -- demonstrating that correlation does not translate to selection is the paper's main insight. Advance (b) is an experimental design choice, not a conceptual advance. Advance (c) is quantitative expansion (more signals) rather than qualitative novelty. Advance (d) is unsubstantiated by the actual experimental results (see W1). The honest differential is primarily advance (a), which is meaningful but possibly thin for a standalone contribution. The SJ metric addressing Nedellec and Xia's surface-matching limitation is a concrete technical advance, but SJ itself is a straightforward application of Hungarian matching with IoU -- not a methodological contribution requiring significant ingenuity.

- **W4: Missing critical related work on constrained decoding and diversity.** The paper claims "first evidence that grammar masking compresses log-prob variance," but several relevant works are absent. Park et al. (2024, "Grammar-Aligned Decoding") discuss how grammar constraints interact with model distributions. Deutsch et al. (2019) study the interaction between constrained decoding and beam diversity in structured prediction. The structured generation community (e.g., Guidance, SGLang) has empirically noted reduced output diversity under strict JSON schemas. The claim of "first evidence" would benefit from a more careful search of this literature. Additionally, the MBR decoding literature (Eikema & Aziz 2022, Freitag et al. 2022, Muller et al. 2021) is cited but the paper does not engage with work on MBR failure modes in low-diversity settings, which is directly relevant.

- **W5: Semantic entropy (Kuhn et al. 2023) deserves deeper engagement as a potential baseline, not just a citation.** The paper mentions semantic entropy in the related work but does not evaluate it or discuss why it was excluded. Semantic entropy clusters generations by meaning and computes entropy over clusters -- this is directly applicable to entity sets (cluster by set overlap, compute entropy). Its omission as a baseline weakens the claim of "systematic" comparison. Similarly, verbalized confidence (Xiong et al. 2024) is cited but not tested; for closed-source API scenarios this is the most natural signal.

- **W6: Scope and generalizability concerns.** The findings are demonstrated only on JSON-schema IE with 8B models. The paper does not discuss whether the same bottleneck applies to other structured generation tasks (code generation with syntax constraints, SQL generation, structured summarization). Code generation under syntax-guided decoding is a natural comparison point where test-time scaling has shown clear gains. If the bottleneck is specific to JSON-schema IE (where the ratio of structural to content tokens is very high), the findings may not generalize. The paper should at least discuss this. Additionally, with only 8B models, it is unclear whether 70B+ models -- which may produce more diverse and higher-quality samples -- would exhibit the same compression.

- **W7: The paper has two identities and needs to commit to one.** The submitted version (docs/paper/) frames the contribution around the correlation-selection gap as a diagnostic finding. The dream version (best/paper.tex) frames it around the constrained decoding bottleneck as a causal finding with free-form ablation and verifier experiments. These are meaningfully different papers. The submitted version is an empirical characterization study. The dream version is a mechanistic investigation. The submitted version's contributions (quality estimation comparison + gap identification + task-complexity boundaries) are individually modest and together feel like a thorough exploratory study rather than a paper with a sharp thesis. The dream version has a sharper thesis but the evidence doesn't support it (W1). The paper needs to find a framing that matches the evidence it actually has.

### Questions for Authors

- **Q1:** The free-form ablation results (freeform_ablation_results.md) show LP variance is essentially identical between constrained and free-form decoding (ratio 0.97x). How do you reconcile this with the claim that constrained decoding *causes* log-prob compression? Could the compression be intrinsic to the fine-tuned model's certainty on these tasks rather than an artifact of grammar masking?

- **Q2:** Have you computed the fraction of generation steps that are deterministic (single valid token) under constrained decoding for your JSON schemas? This would quantify how much of the sequence-level LP is "wasted" on structural tokens. If, say, 80% of tokens are deterministic, this would make the compression mechanism concrete and quantitative.

- **Q3:** Why was semantic entropy not included as a baseline? Entity sets can be clustered by set overlap (using SJ as a similarity kernel), and entropy computed over clusters. This would provide a principled per-instance signal that is conceptually distinct from your five signals.

- **Q4:** The consensus-proximity / MBR selection with SJ achieves 0.645 on NER vs. greedy 0.644 -- essentially identical. Have you tried other utility functions for MBR, such as BERTScore or embedding-based similarity? The failure of SJ-based MBR might reflect the specific utility function rather than a fundamental limitation of MBR for structured IE.

- **Q5:** For the non-tied LP subset (+2.9pp, p=0.01): what distinguishes these instances? Are they longer, more entity-dense, or from specific entity types? Understanding what makes LP non-tied could suggest practical selection strategies.

- **Q6:** The VC signal definition (Eq. 6) aggregates entity frequencies as a QE signal, but for selection you use majority voting (threshold > N/2). Have you tried weighted selection -- selecting the candidate whose entity set has the highest total voting confidence -- as a more natural per-sample adaptation of VC?

### Minor Issues

- The title of the submitted paper ("Strong Predictors, Weak Selectors") is more descriptive but less memorable than the dream version title ("The Constrained Decoding Bottleneck"). However, if the free-form ablation does not support the bottleneck claim, the submitted title is more appropriate.

- Table 1 and the text use slightly inconsistent AUROC values for EM NER (0.749 in Table 1, 0.7478 in freeform results). Ensure consistent rounding throughout.

- The Qwen3 enable_thinking confound is disclosed but its potential impact understated. Empty think blocks still consume part of the training loss budget and may affect first-token distributions. A control experiment (retraining without thinking tokens on SciERC) would be ideal but is acknowledged as infeasible given the timeline.

- Section 2 related work is dense but well-organized. The paragraph on "Consistency for Structured IE" effectively positions against Nedellec and Xia. The connection to MBR decoding (Eikema, Freitag) in the Discussion is strong but could be briefly foreshadowed in Related Work.

- The paper would benefit from a figure showing the log-prob distribution (histogram of within-instance LP ranges) for intuition. The 60% < 0.05 statistic is compelling but a visualization would be more immediate.

- Some bibtex keys in the dream version use placeholder names (e.g., `{UQ Survey}(2025)`, `{CISC}(2025)`) instead of standard author-year keys. The submitted version uses proper keys. Ensure final bibliography is clean.

### Preliminary Scores

- **Soundness: 3.0/5** -- The quality estimation analysis is sound and well-validated. The selection gap is convincingly demonstrated with appropriate statistics. However, the core causal claim (constrained decoding as bottleneck) is either unsubstantiated (submitted version: planned experiment) or contradicted by evidence (actual ablation results). The non-tied subset analysis (p=0.01) provides suggestive evidence but is a subset analysis, not a controlled ablation. The paper is methodologically rigorous within its scope but the central thesis is not adequately supported.

- **Excitement: 2.5/5** -- The quality estimation comparison is a useful empirical contribution but not exciting. The correlation-selection gap is the more interesting finding, but its explanation remains incomplete. If the free-form ablation had confirmed the constrained-decoding hypothesis, this would be a 3.5. As it stands, the paper identifies a problem (selection failure) but does not convincingly explain why it occurs (the proposed mechanism is not confirmed), reducing excitement. The finding that consistency signals are quality estimators but not selectors is useful for practitioners but not conceptually deep.

- **Reproducibility: 4.0/5** -- The paper provides training configurations, hyperparameters, dataset splits, and evaluation protocols in sufficient detail. The use of open-weight models (Qwen3-8B, LLaMA-3.1-8B) and public datasets (SciERC, CoNLL-2003, WNUT-17) supports reproducibility. Code is not mentioned but the methods are straightforward to reimplement.

- **Overall: 2.5/5 (Borderline Findings)** -- The paper makes a genuine empirical contribution in systematically comparing confidence signals for structured IE and identifying the correlation-selection gap. The related work is thorough, the experimental protocol is rigorous, and the writing is generally clear. However, the primary novelty claim -- constrained decoding as a causal bottleneck -- is not supported by the actual ablation evidence, which is a serious gap between the paper's thesis and its data. The remaining contributions (signal comparison, task-complexity boundaries) are useful but incremental. For Findings acceptance, the paper needs to either (a) present the free-form ablation honestly (it did not confirm the hypothesis, which is itself an interesting null result) and reframe accordingly, or (b) find alternative evidence for the bottleneck claim. In its current form, the paper falls between a thorough empirical study without a sharp thesis and a mechanistic investigation without supporting evidence.

- **Confidence: 4/5** -- I am confident in this assessment. I have read the paper carefully, examined the experimental results including the ablation data, and have strong familiarity with the self-consistency, UQ, constrained decoding, and MBR literatures. My main uncertainty is whether the free-form ablation results I examined represent the final version or an intermediate result that may have been updated.
